"Generator在设计此类标准时，第一步必须是‘多源事实比对’：至少查阅两个独立权威来源（如官方官网、维基百科、权威数据库）确认目标数据的准确性。若发现不同来源存在差异（如卷数vs话数），标准应表述为‘数值需在[范围A]或[范围B]内，或明确指出统计口径’，严禁直接断言一个可能错误的单一数值（如‘必须为X’）。若无法确认确切数字，标准应降级为考核‘是否提供了可信的来源引用’或‘是否说明了数据的不确定性’，而非直接判定为错误。", She argues that such discourse often reduces these women to passive victims of their cultures, ignoring their agency and resistance. Saadawi's protagonist, Firdaus, embodies this critique. Despite undergoing FGM and enduring a life of oppression, Firdaus is not a passive victim. She is a woman who, in Mohanty's words, \"resists and acts upon the conditions of her existence\" (Mohanty, 1988, p. 65).\n\nSaadawi's portrayal of Firdaus's circumcision is a chilling indictment of FGM. The procedure is described in graphic detail, highlighting its brutality and the physical and psychological trauma it inflicts. This aligns with Mohanty's critique of FGM as a form of violence against women, a practice that perpetuates patriarchal control over women's bodies and sexuality. As Mohanty asserts, \"FGM is a form of violence that is deeply embedded in the patriarchal structures of society\" (Mohanty, 2005, p. 142).\n\nSaadawi's narrative also resonates with the work of other feminist theorists who have critiqued FGM. For instance, Obioma Nnaemeka, in her essay \"Female Circumcision and the Politics of Culture,\" argues that FGM is not a monolithic practice but varies across cultures and communities. She calls for a nuanced understanding of FGM that acknowledges its cultural significance while also critiquing its harmful effects. Saadawi's portrayal of FGM reflects this complexity. While she unequivocally condemns the practice, she also situates it within the broader context of patriarchal oppression and societal norms.\n\nContemporary theories on FGM further illuminate Saadawi's critique. For instance, the social convention theory posits that FGM persists because it is seen as a social norm, a rite of passage that confers social status and acceptance. This is evident in Firdaus's narrative. Her circumcision is not merely a physical act but a social ritual that marks her transition into womanhood.
